From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR at work and my sister have both been on me about my weight. They keep saying I’ve gotten too thin, like it’s become this whole thing other people are noticing, and I guess it’s gotten hard to brush off when it’s coming from both work and family.\n\nFrom my side, though, I don’t really see it the same way. I mean, my suits fit sharper now, cleaner, and in sales that matters—you’re in front of people all day, you know? But, yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and everyone seems to think that’s a problem. I figured coming in might at least get them to stop worrying so much, or… I don’t know, help me understand if they’re overreacting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… underweight compared to what, I guess? Like, I know everyone is making a big deal out of it, and I can see that I’m smaller than I used to be, sure. People don’t usually get HR involved over nothing, so I’m not pretending nobody has noticed.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to think of it as “underweight” in the way people are saying it, like I’m fragile or in some kind of immediate medical danger. I still jog, I’m still out covering my territory, driving around, meeting clients, standing on warehouse floors, doing presentations, and I’m still hitting my numbers at work. Honestly, in some ways I feel more on top of things because I’m disciplined and my clothes fit cleanly.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Other people keep saying I’m too thin, but it doesn’t feel to me like some emergency or like I can’t function. That’s where I get stuck with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently at a weight that you feel is ideal for yourself?\n\nPatient: Ideal is a tricky word. I guess… not exactly a number, if that’s what you mean. I don’t have some perfect weight written down that I’m trying to hit, at least not in a way I’d say out loud and defend.\n\nIt’s more like how I look in the mirror before I have to be seen by people. Before a meeting, especially, or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check myself and I just sort of lock onto certain things—my stomach, my face. If my face looks fuller or my stomach looks soft, even a little, it gets in my head. I start thinking I look sloppy, like I don’t have myself together, or like people are going to read me as weak. And in my line of work, you’re constantly being judged, even if no one says it directly.\n\nSo when I’m at a weight where my face looks sharper and my clothes sit right and I don’t have that softness around my middle, then, yeah, I feel closer to what I’d call ideal. But I don’t know if that answers your question exactly, because it’s less “this is the ideal weight” and more… I feel okay when I don’t look soft.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like the word “afraid,” because it sounds dramatic, but if I’m being honest, yes, there’s definitely something there.\n\nLike if I go to a client dinner and I have to eat more than I planned, or I’m out for a weekend in Miami and there’s drinks and restaurants and everything is just less controlled, I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even a pound higher, it really gets to me. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, that’s normal fluctuation.” I know people say that, but in the moment it feels like I messed up or like I’m letting things slide.\n\nI get this kind of panic, like I’m losing control of myself. Then my head immediately starts going through what I need to do the next day to fix it—what I should cut back on, what I should avoid, how to get back to where I was. It can take over my whole morning, honestly. So… yeah, I guess I am afraid of gaining weight, even a small amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, I do. I try to make it sound more reasonable in my head, like I’m just being “structured” or “staying disciplined,” but the honest version is that most days I’m definitely limiting it because I don’t want the number to go up.\n\nUsually I’ll just have coffee in the morning, because once I eat breakfast it feels like I’ve opened the door to the whole day going wrong. Then if I’m between sales calls and I’m getting shaky or distracted, I might have a protein bar, something I can count as contained and not a real meal. And then at night I’ll do something small, like grilled chicken, but I’ll skip the rice or anything that feels like it could make me look fuller the next day.\n\nIt’s not that I never get hungry. I do. But a full meal feels… I don’t know, it feels like I’ve messed up the day somehow. Like if I sit down and eat normally, then I’ve lost control, and then I’m going to have to deal with the consequences. So I keep it narrow, because that feels safer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if we’re being objective, yes. It’s been coming down over the past few months.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of why I’m here, or at least why other people pushed me to come here. HR made a comment—not in some official dramatic way at first, more like “Are you okay?”—and then my sister has been on me about it too. She keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, that I don’t look like myself. And I know they’re not saying it out of nowhere, but from my side it doesn’t land that way exactly.\n\nTo me, I look more put together. My suits fit sharper. I don’t feel like they’re pulling in the wrong places, and I look cleaner, more professional, if that makes sense. So when people say “too thin,” I hear that, but I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nThe weight has kept dropping since the divorce, though. I can say that. It wasn’t like one sudden drop overnight, but it’s been steady enough that people have noticed. I just… I don’t know, part of me thinks everyone is overreacting, and part of me knows that if HR and my sister are both bringing it up, maybe it’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you’re asking. I still do my sales route, I still show up, I still make the calls and see accounts and all of that. From the outside I probably look like I’m handling things fine.\n\nBut outside of that, I’ve gotten a lot more… selective, I guess. Or avoidant, if I’m being honest. Client dinners are a big one. If I don’t know the restaurant, or I can’t look at the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those shared-plates situations where everyone’s ordering for the table, I just don’t want to go. I’ll make excuses, like I have another meeting or I’m tired or I need to drive early the next morning.\n\nAnd it’s not just work-related stuff. Dating has gotten weird too. If someone suggests dinner or drinks somewhere I can’t control, I usually back out or try to steer it toward coffee or something “easy.” If I can’t control what the food situation is going to be, I get so preoccupied with it that I’m not even present anyway.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down invitations more. I keep more to myself than I used to. I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy or focused, but a lot of the time it’s really because I don’t want to deal with the food part.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be less attractive or acceptable to others if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds shallow, or like I’m saying everyone else is judging me that harshly, but that is kind of what goes through my head.\n\nBefore a meeting, or especially before a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I won’t really see the whole picture. I’ll just lock onto my stomach or my face. Like, if my shirt sits a certain way, or my face looks fuller that day, I start thinking I look soft. And then that turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m sure other people are going to see that as me being sloppy, or weak, or not as put together.\n\nIt’s not even always about attractiveness in a straightforward way, like “do I look good?” It’s more like… if I gained weight, I’d lose some kind of credibility. Like people would take me less seriously, or I’d seem less disciplined. And with dating, yeah, I worry they’d be less interested. Like whatever edge I have would be gone.\n\nI know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know people would probably say that. My sister definitely would. HR noticing it sort of tells me it’s not just in my head or some private thing. So, objectively, yeah, maybe I’m below what other people would call normal for my height and build.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to feel that way about it. Like, I’m still doing my job. I still drive my territory, I’m still seeing accounts, I’m still hitting my numbers. I can still get up and go for a jog. So when people talk like this is some major medical emergency, part of me just doesn’t connect with that. It feels dramatic, honestly.\n\nI’m not saying there’s nothing going on. I get that everyone is making a big deal about my weight for a reason. But in my own body, day to day, I don’t feel like someone who’s… I don’t know, falling apart. I feel functional. Maybe thinner than before, sure, but not like I’m at some crisis point the way other people seem to think.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or social events that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your concerns about weight or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, I guess I kind of already hinted at that, but yes.\n\nClient dinners are probably the clearest example. I used to actually like them, in a way — not always, because they can be exhausting, but it was part of the job and sometimes it was nice to sit down with an account and have a more normal conversation instead of just talking numbers or product. Now I’ll avoid them if I can’t control the food part. Like if I don’t know where we’re going, or I can’t look up the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those places where everyone orders a bunch of things to share, I get really uncomfortable. I’ll start thinking about it hours before, sometimes the whole day, and then I’m not even focused on the actual meeting.\n\nSo I’ve started making excuses. I’ll say I have an early drive the next morning, or I need to get back, or I already ate, or I’ll try to shift it to coffee instead. And it’s not like I can’t do my job — I still manage my sales route, I still see accounts, I still show up and do the work. But the social side of it has definitely shrunk because of this.\n\nDating too. I used to be more open to just going out to dinner or meeting someone wherever. Now if someone suggests a restaurant and I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering, I’ll either try to steer it somewhere else or I’ll just not go. I’ll tell myself I’m not that interested, or I’m too busy, but if I’m honest, a lot of the time it’s because I don’t want to deal with the menu or the pressure of eating in front of someone.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more than I used to. I don’t love saying that, because it makes it sound like my life is getting smaller, but… that’s probably true.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever engage in any behaviors to compensate for eating, such as self-induced vomiting or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, when you say “compensate,” I know what you’re asking, but I don’t really think of it in that clear cause-and-effect way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to do that.”\n\nI don’t make myself throw up, if that’s what you mean. That’s not something I do. And exercise… I do exercise, but I don’t know where the line is between normal and excessive. Sometimes if I’ve eaten more than I planned, I’ll feel really restless afterward, like I need to do something instead of just sitting there with it. But I don’t always label that as compensating. In my head it’s more like, “I should stay active,” or “I don’t want to feel gross,” which maybe sounds like the same thing from the outside.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no to vomiting. With exercise, I don’t know. I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who’s doing hours and hours every day or anything like that, but I do notice I get uncomfortable if I can’t move around after eating more than I meant to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

zero in on my stomach and face, and if I think I look soft, I feel like people are going to see me as sloppy or weak.\n- key: S084\n  name: Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition\n  description: numbers, so it doesn't feel like some medical emergency to me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I still manage my sales route, but I avoid client dinners and dates unless I can control the menu, so I’ve been turning down invitations and keeping more to myself.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / sales_representative_wholesale_or_manufacturing / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S044\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know people would probably say that. My sister definitely would. HR noticing it sort of tells me it’s not just in my head or some private thing. So, objectively, yeah, maybe I’m below what other people would call normal for my height and build.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to feel that way about it. Like, I’m still doing my job. I still drive my territory, I’m still seeing accounts, I’m still hitting my numbers. I can still get up and go for a jog. So when people talk like this is some major medical emergency, part of me just doesn’t connect with that. It feels dramatic, honestly.\n\nI’m not saying there’s nothing going on. I get that everyone is making a big deal about my weight for a reason. But in my own body, day to day, I don’t feel like someone who’s… I don’t know, falling apart. I feel functional. Maybe thinner than before, sure, but not like I’m at some crisis point the way other people seem to think.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or social events that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your concerns about weight or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, I guess I kind of already hinted at that, but yes.\n\nClient dinners are probably the clearest example. I used to actually like them, in a way — not always, because they can be exhausting, but it was part of the job and sometimes it was nice to sit down with an account and have a more normal conversation instead of just talking numbers or product. Now I’ll avoid them if I can’t control the food part. Like if I don’t know where we’re going, or I can’t look up the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those places where everyone orders a bunch of things to share, I get really uncomfortable. I’ll start thinking about it hours before, sometimes the whole day, and then I’m not even focused on the actual meeting.\n\nSo I’ve started making excuses. I’ll say I have an early drive the next morning, or I need to get back, or I already ate, or I’ll try to shift it to coffee instead. And it’s not like I can’t do my job — I still manage my sales route, I still see accounts, I still show up and do the work. But the social side of it has definitely shrunk because of this.\n\nDating too. I used to be more open to just going out to dinner or meeting someone wherever. Now if someone suggests a restaurant and I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering, I’ll either try to steer it somewhere else or I’ll just not go. I’ll tell myself I’m not that interested, or I’m too busy, but if I’m honest, a lot of the time it’s because I don’t want to deal with the menu or the pressure of eating in front of someone.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more than I used to. I don’t love saying that, because it makes it sound like my life is getting smaller, but… that’s probably true.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever engage in any behaviors to compensate for eating, such as self-induced vomiting or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, when you say “compensate,” I know what you’re asking, but I don’t really think of it in that clear cause-and-effect way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to do that.”\n\nI don’t make myself throw up, if that’s what you mean. That’s not something I do. And exercise… I do exercise, but I don’t know where the line is between normal and excessive. Sometimes if I’ve eaten more than I planned, I’ll feel really restless afterward, like I need to do something instead of just sitting there with it. But I don’t always label that as compensating. In my head it’s more like, “I should stay active,” or “I don’t want to feel gross,” which maybe sounds like the same thing from the outside.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no to vomiting. With exercise, I don’t know. I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who’s doing hours and hours every day or anything like that, but I do notice I get uncomfortable if I can’t move around after eating more than I meant to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help control your weight?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

I mean, my suits fit sharper now, cleaner, and in sales that matters—you’re in front of people all day, you know? I figured coming in might at least get them to stop worrying so much, or… I don’t know, help me understand if they’re overreacting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… underweight compared to what, I guess? Like, I know everyone is making a big deal out of it, and I can see that I’m smaller than I used to be, sure. People don’t usually get HR involved over nothing, so I’m not pretending nobody has noticed.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to think of it as “underweight” in the way people are saying it, like I’m fragile or in some kind of immediate medical danger. I still jog, I’m still out covering my territory, driving around, meeting clients, standing on warehouse floors, doing presentations, and I’m still hitting my numbers at work. Honestly, in some ways I feel more on top of things because I’m disciplined and my clothes fit cleanly.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Other people keep saying I’m too thin, but it doesn’t feel to me like some emergency or like I can’t function. That’s where I get stuck with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently at a weight that you feel is ideal for yourself?\n\nPatient: Ideal is a tricky word. I guess… not exactly a number, if that’s what you mean. I don’t have some perfect weight written down that I’m trying to hit, at least not in a way I’d say out loud and defend.\n\nIt’s more like how I look in the mirror before I have to be seen by people. Before a meeting, especially, or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check myself and I just sort of lock onto certain things—my stomach, my face. If my face looks fuller or my stomach looks soft, even a little, it gets in my head. I start thinking I look sloppy, like I don’t have myself together, or like people are going to read me as weak. And in my line of work, you’re constantly being judged, even if no one says it directly.\n\nSo when I’m at a weight where my face looks sharper and my clothes sit right and I don’t have that softness around my middle, then, yeah, I feel closer to what I’d call ideal. But I don’t know if that answers your question exactly, because it’s less “this is the ideal weight” and more… I feel okay when I don’t look soft.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like the word “afraid,” because it sounds dramatic, but if I’m being honest, yes, there’s definitely something there.\n\nLike if I go to a client dinner and I have to eat more than I planned, or I’m out for a weekend in Miami and there’s drinks and restaurants and everything is just less controlled, I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even a pound higher, it really gets to me. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, that’s normal fluctuation.” I know people say that, but in the moment it feels like I messed up or like I’m letting things slide.\n\nI get this kind of panic, like I’m losing control of myself. Then my head immediately starts going through what I need to do the next day to fix it—what I should cut back on, what I should avoid, how to get back to where I was. It can take over my whole morning, honestly. So… yeah, I guess I am afraid of gaining weight, even a small amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, I do. I try to make it sound more reasonable in my head, like I’m just being “structured” or “staying disciplined,” but the honest version is that most days I’m definitely limiting it because I don’t want the number to go up.\n\nUsually I’ll just have coffee in the morning, because once I eat breakfast it feels like I’ve opened the door to the whole day going wrong. Then if I’m between sales calls and I’m getting shaky or distracted, I might have a protein bar, something I can count as contained and not a real meal. And then at night I’ll do something small, like grilled chicken, but I’ll skip the rice or anything that feels like it could make me look fuller the next day.\n\nIt’s not that I never get hungry. I do. But a full meal feels… I don’t know, it feels like I’ve messed up the day somehow. Like if I sit down and eat normally, then I’ve lost control, and then I’m going to have to deal with the consequences. So I keep it narrow, because that feels safer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if we’re being objective, yes. It’s been coming down over the past few months.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of why I’m here, or at least why other people pushed me to come here. HR made a comment—not in some official dramatic way at first, more like “Are you okay?”—and then my sister has been on me about it too. She keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, that I don’t look like myself. And I know they’re not saying it out of nowhere, but from my side it doesn’t land that way exactly.\n\nTo me, I look more put together. My suits fit sharper. I don’t feel like they’re pulling in the wrong places, and I look cleaner, more professional, if that makes sense. So when people say “too thin,” I hear that, but I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nThe weight has kept dropping since the divorce, though. I can say that. It wasn’t like one sudden drop overnight, but it’s been steady enough that people have noticed. I just… I don’t know, part of me thinks everyone is overreacting, and part of me knows that if HR and my sister are both bringing it up, maybe it’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you’re asking. I still do my sales route, I still show up, I still make the calls and see accounts and all of that. From the outside I probably look like I’m handling things fine.\n\nBut outside of that, I’ve gotten a lot more… selective, I guess. Or avoidant, if I’m being honest. Client dinners are a big one. If I don’t know the restaurant, or I can’t look at the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those shared-plates situations where everyone’s ordering for the table, I just don’t want to go. I’ll make excuses, like I have another meeting or I’m tired or I need to drive early the next morning.\n\nAnd it’s not just work-related stuff. Dating has gotten weird too. If someone suggests dinner or drinks somewhere I can’t control, I usually back out or try to steer it toward coffee or something “easy.” If I can’t control what the food situation is going to be, I get so preoccupied with it that I’m not even present anyway.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down invitations more. I keep more to myself than I used to. I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy or focused, but a lot of the time it’s really because I don’t want to deal with the food part.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be less attractive or acceptable to others if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds shallow, or like I’m saying everyone else is judging me that harshly, but that is kind of what goes through my head.\n\nBefore a meeting, or especially before a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I won’t really see the whole picture. I’ll just lock onto my stomach or my face. Like, if my shirt sits a certain way, or my face looks fuller that day, I start thinking I look soft. I figured coming in might at least get them to stop worrying so much, or… I don’t know, help me understand if they’re overreacting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… underweight compared to what, I guess? Like, I know everyone is making a big deal out of it, and I can see that I’m smaller than I used to be, sure. People don’t usually get HR involved over nothing, so I’m not pretending nobody has noticed.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to think of it as “underweight” in the way people are saying it, like I’m fragile or in some kind of immediate medical danger. I still jog, I’m still out covering my territory, driving around, meeting clients, standing on warehouse floors, doing presentations, and I’m still hitting my numbers at work. Honestly, in some ways I feel more on top of things because I’m disciplined and my clothes fit cleanly.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Other people keep saying I’m too thin, but it doesn’t feel to me like some emergency or like I can’t function. That’s where I get stuck with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently at a weight that you feel is ideal for yourself?\n\nPatient: Ideal is a tricky word. I guess… not exactly a number, if that’s what you mean. I don’t have some perfect weight written down that I’m trying to hit, at least not in a way I’d say out loud and defend.\n\nIt’s more like how I look in the mirror before I have to be seen by people. Before a meeting, especially, or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check myself and I just sort of lock onto certain things—my stomach, my face. If my face looks fuller or my stomach looks soft, even a little, it gets in my head. I start thinking I look sloppy, like I don’t have myself together, or like people are going to read me as weak. And in my line of work, you’re constantly being judged, even if no one says it directly.\n\nSo when I’m at a weight where my face looks sharper and my clothes sit right and I don’t have that softness around my middle, then, yeah, I feel closer to what I’d call ideal. But I don’t know if that answers your question exactly, because it’s less “this is the ideal weight” and more… I feel okay when I don’t look soft.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like the word “afraid,” because it sounds dramatic, but if I’m being honest, yes, there’s definitely something there.\n\nLike if I go to a client dinner and I have to eat more than I planned, or I’m out for a weekend in Miami and there’s drinks and restaurants and everything is just less controlled, I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even a pound higher, it really gets to me. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, that’s normal fluctuation.” I know people say that, but in the moment it feels like I messed up or like I’m letting things slide.\n\nI get this kind of panic, like I’m losing control of myself. Then my head immediately starts going through what I need to do the next day to fix it—what I should cut back on, what I should avoid, how to get back to where I was. It can take over my whole morning, honestly. So… yeah, I guess I am afraid of gaining weight, even a small amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, I do. I try to make it sound more reasonable in my head, like I’m just being “structured” or “staying disciplined,” but the honest version is that most days I’m definitely limiting it because I don’t want the number to go up.\n\nUsually I’ll just have coffee in the morning, because once I eat breakfast it feels like I’ve opened the door to the whole day going wrong. Then if I’m between sales calls and I’m getting shaky or distracted, I might have a protein bar, something I can count as contained and not a real meal. And then at night I’ll do something small, like grilled chicken, but I’ll skip the rice or anything that feels like it could make me look fuller the next day.\n\nIt’s not that I never get hungry. I do. But a full meal feels… I don’t know, it feels like I’ve messed up the day somehow. Like if I sit down and eat normally, then I’ve lost control, and then I’m going to have to deal with the consequences. So I keep it narrow, because that feels safer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if we’re being objective, yes. It’s been coming down over the past few months.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of why I’m here, or at least why other people pushed me to come here. HR made a comment—not in some official dramatic way at first, more like “Are you okay?”—and then my sister has been on me about it too. She keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, that I don’t look like myself. And I know they’re not saying it out of nowhere, but from my side it doesn’t land that way exactly.\n\nTo me, I look more put together. My suits fit sharper. I don’t feel like they’re pulling in the wrong places, and I look cleaner, more professional, if that makes sense. So when people say “too thin,” I hear that, but I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nThe weight has kept dropping since the divorce, though. I can say that. It wasn’t like one sudden drop overnight, but it’s been steady enough that people have noticed. I just… I don’t know, part of me thinks everyone is overreacting, and part of me knows that if HR and my sister are both bringing it up, maybe it’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you’re asking. I still do my sales route, I still show up, I still make the calls and see accounts and all of that. From the outside I probably look like I’m handling things fine.\n\nBut outside of that, I’ve gotten a lot more… selective, I guess. Or avoidant, if I’m being honest. Client dinners are a big one. If I don’t know the restaurant, or I can’t look at the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those shared-plates situations where everyone’s ordering for the table, I just don’t want to go. I’ll make excuses, like I have another meeting or I’m tired or I need to drive early the next morning.\n\nAnd it’s not just work-related stuff. Dating has gotten weird too. If someone suggests dinner or drinks somewhere I can’t control, I usually back out or try to steer it toward coffee or something “easy.” If I can’t control what the food situation is going to be, I get so preoccupied with it that I’m not even present anyway.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down invitations more. I keep more to myself than I used to. I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy or focused, but a lot of the time it’s really because I don’t want to deal with the food part.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be less attractive or acceptable to others if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds shallow, or like I’m saying everyone else is judging me that harshly, but that is kind of what goes through my head.\n\nBefore a meeting, or especially before a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I won’t really see the whole picture. I’ll just lock onto my stomach or my face. Like, if my shirt sits a certain way, or my face looks fuller that day, I start thinking I look soft. And then that turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m sure other people are going to see that as me being sloppy, or weak, or not as put together.\n\nIt’s not even always about attractiveness in a straightforward way, like “do I look good?” It’s more like… if I gained weight, I’d lose some kind of credibility. Like people would take me less seriously, or I’d seem less disciplined. And with dating, yeah, I worry they’d be less interested. Like whatever edge I have would be gone.\n\nI know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know people would probably say that. My sister definitely would. HR noticing it sort of tells me it’s not just in my head or some private thing. So, objectively, yeah, maybe I’m below what other people would call normal for my height and build.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to feel that way about it. Like, I’m still doing my job. I still drive my territory, I’m still seeing accounts, I’m still hitting my numbers. I can still get up and go for a jog. So when people talk like this is some major medical emergency, part of me just doesn’t connect with that. It feels dramatic, honestly.\n\nI’m not saying there’s nothing going on. I get that everyone is making a big deal about my weight for a reason. But in my own body, day to day, I don’t feel like someone who’s… I don’t know, falling apart. I feel functional. Maybe thinner than before, sure, but not like I’m at some crisis point the way other people seem to think.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or social events that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your concerns about weight or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, I guess I kind of already hinted at that, but yes.\n\nClient dinners are probably the clearest example. I used to actually like them, in a way — not always, because they can be exhausting, but it was part of the job and sometimes it was nice to sit down with an account and have a more normal conversation instead of just talking numbers or product. Now I’ll avoid them if I can’t control the food part. Like if I don’t know where we’re going, or I can’t look up the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those places where everyone orders a bunch of things to share, I get really uncomfortable. I’ll start thinking about it hours before, sometimes the whole day, and then I’m not even focused on the actual meeting.\n\nSo I’ve started making excuses. I’ll say I have an early drive the next morning, or I need to get back, or I already ate, or I’ll try to shift it to coffee instead. And it’s not like I can’t do my job — I still manage my sales route, I still see accounts, I still show up and do the work. But the social side of it has definitely shrunk because of this.\n\nDating too. I used to be more open to just going out to dinner or meeting someone wherever. Now if someone suggests a restaurant and I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering, I’ll either try to steer it somewhere else or I’ll just not go. I’ll tell myself I’m not that interested, or I’m too busy, but if I’m honest, a lot of the time it’s because I don’t want to deal with the menu or the pressure of eating in front of someone.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more than I used to. I don’t love saying that, because it makes it sound like my life is getting smaller, but… that’s probably true.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever engage in any behaviors to compensate for eating, such as self-induced vomiting or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, when you say “compensate,” I know what you’re asking, but I don’t really think of it in that clear cause-and-effect way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to do that.”\n\nI don’t make myself throw up, if that’s what you mean. That’s not something I do. And exercise… I do exercise, but I don’t know where the line is between normal and excessive. Sometimes if I’ve eaten more than I planned, I’ll feel really restless afterward, like I need to do something instead of just sitting there with it. But I don’t always label that as compensating. In my head it’s more like, “I should stay active,” or “I don’t want to feel gross,” which maybe sounds like the same thing from the outside.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no to vomiting. With exercise, I don’t know. I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who’s doing hours and hours every day or anything like that, but I do notice I get uncomfortable if I can’t move around after eating more than I meant to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what they are, obviously, but I don’t use them for that. I don’t have some routine where I’m taking pills or water pills or anything to make the number go down.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because when you ask it that way, I start second-guessing whether you mean prescription stuff, over-the-counter stuff, teas, supplements, all of that. But no — I’m not doing that as a weight-control thing. That’s not part of what I do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes. I don’t really like admitting that, because part of me wants to say, “I’m fine, I’m still functioning,” and technically I am. I still get up, I still manage my sales route, I still meet with accounts and do what I’m supposed to do for work. From the outside, it probably doesn’t look like everything has changed.

But it has affected my life. It’s like my whole day can start bending around whether I’ll be able to control what I eat, or what the options are going to be. Client dinners are the big one. I’ll avoid them unless I know the restaurant, or I can look at the menu ahead of time, or I feel like I can order something that feels… safe, I guess. If I can’t do that, I’ll come up with a reason not to go, or I’ll try to turn it into coffee or a quick meeting instead.

And dating has gotten harder too. I’ll turn down plans or avoid making them if they involve dinner somewhere I don’t know. I tell myself it’s because I’m busy or not that interested, but a lot of the time it’s really about the food part. So I’ve been saying no to more things, keeping more to myself.

So, yeah, I’d say it’s affecting my happiness. Maybe not in some dramatic way every second, but my world feels smaller than it used to, and I don’t feel great about that.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR at work and my sister have both been on me about my weight. They keep saying I’ve gotten too thin, like it’s become this whole thing other people are noticing, and I guess it’s gotten hard to brush off when it’s coming from both work and family.\n\nFrom my side, though, I don’t really see it the same way. I mean, my suits fit sharper now, cleaner, and in sales that matters—you’re in front of people all day, you know? But, yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and everyone seems to think that’s a problem. I figured coming in might at least get them to stop worrying so much, or… I don’t know, help me understand if they’re overreacting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… underweight compared to what, I guess? Like, I know everyone is making a big deal out of it, and I can see that I’m smaller than I used to be, sure. People don’t usually get HR involved over nothing, so I’m not pretending nobody has noticed.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to think of it as “underweight” in the way people are saying it, like I’m fragile or in some kind of immediate medical danger. I still jog, I’m still out covering my territory, driving around, meeting clients, standing on warehouse floors, doing presentations, and I’m still hitting my numbers at work. Honestly, in some ways I feel more on top of things because I’m disciplined and my clothes fit cleanly.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Other people keep saying I’m too thin, but it doesn’t feel to me like some emergency or like I can’t function. That’s where I get stuck with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently at a weight that you feel is ideal for yourself?\n\nPatient: Ideal is a tricky word. I guess… not exactly a number, if that’s what you mean. I don’t have some perfect weight written down that I’m trying to hit, at least not in a way I’d say out loud and defend.\n\nIt’s more like how I look in the mirror before I have to be seen by people. Before a meeting, especially, or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check myself and I just sort of lock onto certain things—my stomach, my face. If my face looks fuller or my stomach looks soft, even a little, it gets in my head. I start thinking I look sloppy, like I don’t have myself together, or like people are going to read me as weak. And in my line of work, you’re constantly being judged, even if no one says it directly.\n\nSo when I’m at a weight where my face looks sharper and my clothes sit right and I don’t have that softness around my middle, then, yeah, I feel closer to what I’d call ideal. But I don’t know if that answers your question exactly, because it’s less “this is the ideal weight” and more… I feel okay when I don’t look soft.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like the word “afraid,” because it sounds dramatic, but if I’m being honest, yes, there’s definitely something there.\n\nLike if I go to a client dinner and I have to eat more than I planned, or I’m out for a weekend in Miami and there’s drinks and restaurants and everything is just less controlled, I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even a pound higher, it really gets to me. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, that’s normal fluctuation.” I know people say that, but in the moment it feels like I messed up or like I’m letting things slide.\n\nI get this kind of panic, like I’m losing control of myself. Then my head immediately starts going through what I need to do the next day to fix it—what I should cut back on, what I should avoid, how to get back to where I was. It can take over my whole morning, honestly. So… yeah, I guess I am afraid of gaining weight, even a small amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, I do. I try to make it sound more reasonable in my head, like I’m just being “structured” or “staying disciplined,” but the honest version is that most days I’m definitely limiting it because I don’t want the number to go up.\n\nUsually I’ll just have coffee in the morning, because once I eat breakfast it feels like I’ve opened the door to the whole day going wrong. Then if I’m between sales calls and I’m getting shaky or distracted, I might have a protein bar, something I can count as contained and not a real meal. And then at night I’ll do something small, like grilled chicken, but I’ll skip the rice or anything that feels like it could make me look fuller the next day.\n\nIt’s not that I never get hungry. I do. But a full meal feels… I don’t know, it feels like I’ve messed up the day somehow. Like if I sit down and eat normally, then I’ve lost control, and then I’m going to have to deal with the consequences. So I keep it narrow, because that feels safer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if we’re being objective, yes. It’s been coming down over the past few months.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of why I’m here, or at least why other people pushed me to come here. HR made a comment—not in some official dramatic way at first, more like “Are you okay?”—and then my sister has been on me about it too. She keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, that I don’t look like myself. And I know they’re not saying it out of nowhere, but from my side it doesn’t land that way exactly.\n\nTo me, I look more put together. My suits fit sharper. I don’t feel like they’re pulling in the wrong places, and I look cleaner, more professional, if that makes sense. So when people say “too thin,” I hear that, but I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nThe weight has kept dropping since the divorce, though. I can say that. It wasn’t like one sudden drop overnight, but it’s been steady enough that people have noticed. I just… I don’t know, part of me thinks everyone is overreacting, and part of me knows that if HR and my sister are both bringing it up, maybe it’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you’re asking. I still do my sales route, I still show up, I still make the calls and see accounts and all of that. From the outside I probably look like I’m handling things fine.\n\nBut outside of that, I’ve gotten a lot more… selective, I guess. Or avoidant, if I’m being honest. Client dinners are a big one. If I don’t know the restaurant, or I can’t look at the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those shared-plates situations where everyone’s ordering for the table, I just don’t want to go. I’ll make excuses, like I have another meeting or I’m tired or I need to drive early the next morning.\n\nAnd it’s not just work-related stuff. Dating has gotten weird too. If someone suggests dinner or drinks somewhere I can’t control, I usually back out or try to steer it toward coffee or something “easy.” If I can’t control what the food situation is going to be, I get so preoccupied with it that I’m not even present anyway.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down invitations more. I keep more to myself than I used to. I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy or focused, but a lot of the time it’s really because I don’t want to deal with the food part.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be less attractive or acceptable to others if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds shallow, or like I’m saying everyone else is judging me that harshly, but that is kind of what goes through my head.\n\nBefore a meeting, or especially before a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I won’t really see the whole picture. I’ll just lock onto my stomach or my face. Like, if my shirt sits a certain way, or my face looks fuller that day, I start thinking I look soft. And then that turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m sure other people are going to see that as me being sloppy, or weak, or not as put together.\n\nIt’s not even always about attractiveness in a straightforward way, like “do I look good?” It’s more like… if I gained weight, I’d lose some kind of credibility. Like people would take me less seriously, or I’d seem less disciplined. And with dating, yeah, I worry they’d be less interested. Like whatever edge I have would be gone.\n\nI know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know people would probably say that. My sister definitely would. HR noticing it sort of tells me it’s not just in my head or some private thing. So, objectively, yeah, maybe I’m below what other people would call normal for my height and build.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to feel that way about it. Like, I’m still doing my job. I still drive my territory, I’m still seeing accounts, I’m still hitting my numbers. I can still get up and go for a jog. So when people talk like this is some major medical emergency, part of me just doesn’t connect with that. It feels dramatic, honestly.\n\nI’m not saying there’s nothing going on. I get that everyone is making a big deal about my weight for a reason. But in my own body, day to day, I don’t feel like someone who’s… I don’t know, falling apart. I feel functional. Maybe thinner than before, sure, but not like I’m at some crisis point the way other people seem to think.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or social events that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your concerns about weight or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, I guess I kind of already hinted at that, but yes.\n\nClient dinners are probably the clearest example. I used to actually like them, in a way — not always, because they can be exhausting, but it was part of the job and sometimes it was nice to sit down with an account and have a more normal conversation instead of just talking numbers or product. Now I’ll avoid them if I can’t control the food part. Like if I don’t know where we’re going, or I can’t look up the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those places where everyone orders a bunch of things to share, I get really uncomfortable. I’ll start thinking about it hours before, sometimes the whole day, and then I’m not even focused on the actual meeting.\n\nSo I’ve started making excuses. I’ll say I have an early drive the next morning, or I need to get back, or I already ate, or I’ll try to shift it to coffee instead. And it’s not like I can’t do my job — I still manage my sales route, I still see accounts, I still show up and do the work. But the social side of it has definitely shrunk because of this.\n\nDating too. I used to be more open to just going out to dinner or meeting someone wherever. Now if someone suggests a restaurant and I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering, I’ll either try to steer it somewhere else or I’ll just not go. I’ll tell myself I’m not that interested, or I’m too busy, but if I’m honest, a lot of the time it’s because I don’t want to deal with the menu or the pressure of eating in front of someone.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more than I used to. I don’t love saying that, because it makes it sound like my life is getting smaller, but… that’s probably true.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever engage in any behaviors to compensate for eating, such as self-induced vomiting or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, when you say “compensate,” I know what you’re asking, but I don’t really think of it in that clear cause-and-effect way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to do that.”\n\nI don’t make myself throw up, if that’s what you mean. That’s not something I do. And exercise… I do exercise, but I don’t know where the line is between normal and excessive. Sometimes if I’ve eaten more than I planned, I’ll feel really restless afterward, like I need to do something instead of just sitting there with it. But I don’t always label that as compensating. In my head it’s more like, “I should stay active,” or “I don’t want to feel gross,” which maybe sounds like the same thing from the outside.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no to vomiting. With exercise, I don’t know. I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who’s doing hours and hours every day or anything like that, but I do notice I get uncomfortable if I can’t move around after eating more than I meant to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what they are, obviously, but I don’t use them for that. I don’t have some routine where I’m taking pills or water pills or anything to make the number go down.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because when you ask it that way, I start second-guessing whether you mean prescription stuff, over-the-counter stuff, teas, supplements, all of that. But no — I’m not doing that as a weight-control thing. That’s not part of what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about weight are affecting your overall well-being or happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I don’t really like admitting that, because part of me wants to say, “I’m fine, I’m still functioning,” and technically I am. I still get up, I still manage my sales route, I still meet with accounts and do what I’m supposed to do for work. From the outside, it probably doesn’t look like everything has changed.\n\nBut it has affected my life. It’s like my whole day can start bending around whether I’ll be able to control what I eat, or what the options are going to be. Client dinners are the big one. I’ll avoid them unless I know the restaurant, or I can look at the menu ahead of time, or I feel like I can order something that feels… safe, I guess. If I can’t do that, I’ll come up with a reason not to go, or I’ll try to turn it into coffee or a quick meeting instead.\n\nAnd dating has gotten harder too. I’ll turn down plans or avoid making them if they involve dinner somewhere I don’t know. I tell myself it’s because I’m busy or not that interested, but a lot of the time it’s really about the food part. So I’ve been saying no to more things, keeping more to myself.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s affecting my happiness. Maybe not in some dramatic way every second, but my world feels smaller than it used to, and I don’t feel great about that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Yeah… I think it is. I don’t always call it “fear of gaining weight” in the moment, because I sort of dress it up as being practical or busy or needing to stay on schedule, but if I’m being honest, it’s there.

I can still do my job. I still manage my sales route, I still show up for accounts, I still get through the day. So part of me wants to say it hasn’t really stopped me from functioning. But the things around work — the social parts, the dinners, the after-hours stuff — those have gotten a lot harder. If a client suggests dinner and I don’t know the place, or I can’t look at the menu first, or I don’t feel like I’ll have control over what I order, I’ll find a way around it. I’ll say I have another appointment, or suggest coffee instead, or try to keep it to a meeting during the day.

And it’s the same with dating, honestly. If it involves dinner somewhere I don’t know, I get tense about it before I even answer. I’ll turn things down that I probably would have enjoyed before, or I’ll avoid making plans unless I know I can control the food situation. So it’s not like I’m sitting at home all day unable to do anything, but I’ve definitely been saying no more. I keep more to myself. My life has gotten smaller in this way that I don’t really like admitting.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR at work and my sister have both been on me about my weight. They keep saying I’ve gotten too thin, like it’s become this whole thing other people are noticing, and I guess it’s gotten hard to brush off when it’s coming from both work and family.\n\nFrom my side, though, I don’t really see it the same way. But, yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and everyone seems to think that’s a problem. Like, I know everyone is making a big deal out of it, and I can see that I’m smaller than I used to be, sure. People don’t usually get HR involved over nothing, so I’m not pretending nobody has noticed.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to think of it as “underweight” in the way people are saying it, like I’m fragile or in some kind of immediate medical danger. I still jog, I’m still out covering my territory, driving around, meeting clients, standing on warehouse floors, doing presentations, and I’m still hitting my numbers at work. Honestly, in some ways I feel more on top of things because I’m disciplined and my clothes fit cleanly.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Other people keep saying I’m too thin, but it doesn’t feel to me like some emergency or like I can’t function. I guess… not exactly a number, if that’s what you mean. I don’t have some perfect weight written down that I’m trying to hit, at least not in a way I’d say out loud and defend.\n\nIt’s more like how I look in the mirror before I have to be seen by people. Before a meeting, especially, or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check myself and I just sort of lock onto certain things—my stomach, my face. If my face looks fuller or my stomach looks soft, even a little, it gets in my head. I start thinking I look sloppy, like I don’t have myself together, or like people are going to read me as weak. And in my line of work, you’re constantly being judged, even if no one says it directly.\n\nSo when I’m at a weight where my face looks sharper and my clothes sit right and I don’t have that softness around my middle, then, yeah, I feel closer to what I’d call ideal. But I don’t know if that answers your question exactly, because it’s less “this is the ideal weight” and more… I feel okay when I don’t look soft.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like the word “afraid,” because it sounds dramatic, but if I’m being honest, yes, there’s definitely something there.\n\nLike if I go to a client dinner and I have to eat more than I planned, or I’m out for a weekend in Miami and there’s drinks and restaurants and everything is just less controlled, I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even a pound higher, it really gets to me. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, that’s normal fluctuation.” I know people say that, but in the moment it feels like I messed up or like I’m letting things slide.\n\nI get this kind of panic, like I’m losing control of myself. Then my head immediately starts going through what I need to do the next day to fix it—what I should cut back on, what I should avoid, how to get back to where I was. It can take over my whole morning, honestly. So… yeah, I guess I am afraid of gaining weight, even a small amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, I do. I try to make it sound more reasonable in my head, like I’m just being “structured” or “staying disciplined,” but the honest version is that most days I’m definitely limiting it because I don’t want the number to go up.\n\nUsually I’ll just have coffee in the morning, because once I eat breakfast it feels like I’ve opened the door to the whole day going wrong. Then if I’m between sales calls and I’m getting shaky or distracted, I might have a protein bar, something I can count as contained and not a real meal. And then at night I’ll do something small, like grilled chicken, but I’ll skip the rice or anything that feels like it could make me look fuller the next day.\n\nIt’s not that I never get hungry. I do. But a full meal feels… I don’t know, it feels like I’ve messed up the day somehow. Like if I sit down and eat normally, then I’ve lost control, and then I’m going to have to deal with the consequences. So I keep it narrow, because that feels safer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if we’re being objective, yes. It’s been coming down over the past few months.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of why I’m here, or at least why other people pushed me to come here. HR made a comment—not in some official dramatic way at first, more like “Are you okay?”—and then my sister has been on me about it too. She keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, that I don’t look like myself. And I know they’re not saying it out of nowhere, but from my side it doesn’t land that way exactly.\n\nTo me, I look more put together. My suits fit sharper. I don’t feel like they’re pulling in the wrong places, and I look cleaner, more professional, if that makes sense. So when people say “too thin,” I hear that, but I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nThe weight has kept dropping since the divorce, though. I can say that. It wasn’t like one sudden drop overnight, but it’s been steady enough that people have noticed. I just… I don’t know, part of me thinks everyone is overreacting, and part of me knows that if HR and my sister are both bringing it up, maybe it’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you’re asking. I still do my sales route, I still show up, I still make the calls and see accounts and all of that. From the outside I probably look like I’m handling things fine.\n\nBut outside of that, I’ve gotten a lot more… selective, I guess. Or avoidant, if I’m being honest. Client dinners are a big one. If I don’t know the restaurant, or I can’t look at the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those shared-plates situations where everyone’s ordering for the table, I just don’t want to go. I’ll make excuses, like I have another meeting or I’m tired or I need to drive early the next morning.\n\nAnd it’s not just work-related stuff. Dating has gotten weird too. If someone suggests dinner or drinks somewhere I can’t control, I usually back out or try to steer it toward coffee or something “easy.” If I can’t control what the food situation is going to be, I get so preoccupied with it that I’m not even present anyway.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down invitations more. I keep more to myself than I used to. I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy or focused, but a lot of the time it’s really because I don’t want to deal with the food part.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be less attractive or acceptable to others if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds shallow, or like I’m saying everyone else is judging me that harshly, but that is kind of what goes through my head.\n\nBefore a meeting, or especially before a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I won’t really see the whole picture. I’ll just lock onto my stomach or my face. Like, if my shirt sits a certain way, or my face looks fuller that day, I start thinking I look soft. And then that turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m sure other people are going to see that as me being sloppy, or weak, or not as put together.\n\nIt’s not even always about attractiveness in a straightforward way, like “do I look good?” It’s more like… if I gained weight, I’d lose some kind of credibility. Like people would take me less seriously, or I’d seem less disciplined. And with dating, yeah, I worry they’d be less interested. Like whatever edge I have would be gone.\n\nI know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know people would probably say that. My sister definitely would. HR noticing it sort of tells me it’s not just in my head or some private thing. So, objectively, yeah, maybe I’m below what other people would call normal for my height and build.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to feel that way about it. Like, I’m still doing my job. I still drive my territory, I’m still seeing accounts, I’m still hitting my numbers. I can still get up and go for a jog. So when people talk like this is some major medical emergency, part of me just doesn’t connect with that. It feels dramatic, honestly.\n\nI’m not saying there’s nothing going on. I get that everyone is making a big deal about my weight for a reason. But in my own body, day to day, I don’t feel like someone who’s… I don’t know, falling apart. I feel functional. Maybe thinner than before, sure, but not like I’m at some crisis point the way other people seem to think.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or social events that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your concerns about weight or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, I guess I kind of already hinted at that, but yes.\n\nClient dinners are probably the clearest example. I used to actually like them, in a way — not always, because they can be exhausting, but it was part of the job and sometimes it was nice to sit down with an account and have a more normal conversation instead of just talking numbers or product. Now I’ll avoid them if I can’t control the food part. Like if I don’t know where we’re going, or I can’t look up the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those places where everyone orders a bunch of things to share, I get really uncomfortable. I’ll start thinking about it hours before, sometimes the whole day, and then I’m not even focused on the actual meeting.\n\nSo I’ve started making excuses. I’ll say I have an early drive the next morning, or I need to get back, or I already ate, or I’ll try to shift it to coffee instead. And it’s not like I can’t do my job — I still manage my sales route, I still see accounts, I still show up and do the work. But the social side of it has definitely shrunk because of this.\n\nDating too. I used to be more open to just going out to dinner or meeting someone wherever. Now if someone suggests a restaurant and I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering, I’ll either try to steer it somewhere else or I’ll just not go. I’ll tell myself I’m not that interested, or I’m too busy, but if I’m honest, a lot of the time it’s because I don’t want to deal with the menu or the pressure of eating in front of someone.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more than I used to. I don’t love saying that, because it makes it sound like my life is getting smaller, but… that’s probably true.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever engage in any behaviors to compensate for eating, such as self-induced vomiting or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, when you say “compensate,” I know what you’re asking, but I don’t really think of it in that clear cause-and-effect way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to do that.”\n\nI don’t make myself throw up, if that’s what you mean. That’s not something I do. And exercise… I do exercise, but I don’t know where the line is between normal and excessive. Sometimes if I’ve eaten more than I planned, I’ll feel really restless afterward, like I need to do something instead of just sitting there with it. But I don’t always label that as compensating. In my head it’s more like, “I should stay active,” or “I don’t want to feel gross,” which maybe sounds like the same thing from the outside.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no to vomiting. With exercise, I don’t know. I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who’s doing hours and hours every day or anything like that, but I do notice I get uncomfortable if I can’t move around after eating more than I meant to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what they are, obviously, but I don’t use them for that. I don’t have some routine where I’m taking pills or water pills or anything to make the number go down.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because when you ask it that way, I start second-guessing whether you mean prescription stuff, over-the-counter stuff, teas, supplements, all of that. But no — I’m not doing that as a weight-control thing. That’s not part of what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about weight are affecting your overall well-being or happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I don’t really like admitting that, because part of me wants to say, “I’m fine, I’m still functioning,” and technically I am. I still get up, I still manage my sales route, I still meet with accounts and do what I’m supposed to do for work. From the outside, it probably doesn’t look like everything has changed.\n\nBut it has affected my life. It’s like my whole day can start bending around whether I’ll be able to control what I eat, or what the options are going to be. Client dinners are the big one. I’ll avoid them unless I know the restaurant, or I can look at the menu ahead of time, or I feel like I can order something that feels… safe, I guess. If I can’t do that, I’ll come up with a reason not to go, or I’ll try to turn it into coffee or a quick meeting instead.\n\nAnd dating has gotten harder too. I’ll turn down plans or avoid making them if they involve dinner somewhere I don’t know. I tell myself it’s because I’m busy or not that interested, but a lot of the time it’s really about the food part. So I’ve been saying no to more things, keeping more to myself.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s affecting my happiness. Maybe not in some dramatic way every second, but my world feels smaller than it used to, and I don’t feel great about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of gaining weight is impacting your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I don’t always call it “fear of gaining weight” in the moment, because I sort of dress it up as being practical or busy or needing to stay on schedule, but if I’m being honest, it’s there.\n\nI can still do my job. I still manage my sales route, I still show up for accounts, I still get through the day. So part of me wants to say it hasn’t really stopped me from functioning. But the things around work — the social parts, the dinners, the after-hours stuff — those have gotten a lot harder. If a client suggests dinner and I don’t know the place, or I can’t look at the menu first, or I don’t feel like I’ll have control over what I order, I’ll find a way around it. I’ll say I have another appointment, or suggest coffee instead, or try to keep it to a meeting during the day.\n\nAnd it’s the same with dating, honestly. If it involves dinner somewhere I don’t know, I get tense about it before I even answer. I’ll turn things down that I probably would have enjoyed before, or I’ll avoid making plans unless I know I can control the food situation. So it’s not like I’m sitting at home all day unable to do anything, but I’ve definitely been saying no more. I keep more to myself. My life has gotten smaller in this way that I don’t really like admitting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
