I don’t really like admitting that, because it sounds… I don’t know, kind of controlled by something that should be simple. But if someone wants to go to dinner somewhere I don’t know, I’ll hesitate or turn it down, even if I might actually want to see them. I’ll tell myself I’m busy or it’s not a good night, but really it’s that I don’t want to be stuck with a menu I can’t manage.\n\nSo, yes, it’s made my life smaller. I’m turning down more invitations, avoiding things that involve meals, and keeping more to myself than I used to. It’s not like I can’t function at all, but I’m definitely not enjoying or participating in things the way I used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / sales_representative_wholesale_or_manufacturing / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"When I look in the mirror before a meeting or a date, I zero in on my stomach and face, and if I think I look soft, I feel like people are going to see me as sloppy or weak.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think probably yes. It’s hard to answer because part of me wants to say, “No, I’m just seeing what’s actually there,” but then I also know I get really fixated on certain parts in a way that maybe other people don’t.

Like before a meeting or before a date, I’ll check myself in the mirror, and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my shirt sits a certain way or my face looks even a little fuller to me, then that becomes the only thing I can see. I’ll think I look soft, or kind of sloppy, even if objectively I’ve looked basically the same as I did the day before.

And then I start assuming other people are going to see that too. Like a client will look at me and think I’m not disciplined, or I’m weak, or I don’t have myself together. With dating it’s even worse, because I’ll imagine the person noticing my stomach or my face and judging me before I’ve even walked in.

I don’t know if other people actually see what I’m seeing. Nobody is saying those things to me. But in the moment, it feels very real, like it’s obvious and I’m just the one willing to admit it. Then later I can sometimes see that maybe I was over-focused on it, but when I’m standing there looking in the mirror, it’s hard to believe anyone else would see me differently.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR at work and my sister have both been on me about my weight. They keep saying I’ve gotten too thin, like it’s become this whole thing other people are noticing, and I guess it’s gotten hard to brush off when it’s coming from both work and family.\n\nFrom my side, though, I don’t really see it the same way. I mean, my suits fit sharper now, cleaner, and in sales that matters—you’re in front of people all day, you know? But, yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and everyone seems to think that’s a problem. I figured coming in might at least get them to stop worrying so much, or… I don’t know, help me understand if they’re overreacting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… underweight compared to what, I guess? Like, I know everyone is making a big deal out of it, and I can see that I’m smaller than I used to be, sure. People don’t usually get HR involved over nothing, so I’m not pretending nobody has noticed.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to think of it as “underweight” in the way people are saying it, like I’m fragile or in some kind of immediate medical danger. I still jog, I’m still out covering my territory, driving around, meeting clients, standing on warehouse floors, doing presentations, and I’m still hitting my numbers at work. Honestly, in some ways I feel more on top of things because I’m disciplined and my clothes fit cleanly.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Other people keep saying I’m too thin, but it doesn’t feel to me like some emergency or like I can’t function. That’s where I get stuck with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently at a weight that you feel is ideal for yourself?\n\nPatient: Ideal is a tricky word. I guess… not exactly a number, if that’s what you mean. I don’t have some perfect weight written down that I’m trying to hit, at least not in a way I’d say out loud and defend.\n\nIt’s more like how I look in the mirror before I have to be seen by people. Before a meeting, especially, or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check myself and I just sort of lock onto certain things—my stomach, my face. If my face looks fuller or my stomach looks soft, even a little, it gets in my head. I start thinking I look sloppy, like I don’t have myself together, or like people are going to read me as weak. And in my line of work, you’re constantly being judged, even if no one says it directly.\n\nSo when I’m at a weight where my face looks sharper and my clothes sit right and I don’t have that softness around my middle, then, yeah, I feel closer to what I’d call ideal. But I don’t know if that answers your question exactly, because it’s less “this is the ideal weight” and more… I feel okay when I don’t look soft.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like the word “afraid,” because it sounds dramatic, but if I’m being honest, yes, there’s definitely something there.\n\nLike if I go to a client dinner and I have to eat more than I planned, or I’m out for a weekend in Miami and there’s drinks and restaurants and everything is just less controlled, I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even a pound higher, it really gets to me. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, that’s normal fluctuation.” I know people say that, but in the moment it feels like I messed up or like I’m letting things slide.\n\nI get this kind of panic, like I’m losing control of myself. Then my head immediately starts going through what I need to do the next day to fix it—what I should cut back on, what I should avoid, how to get back to where I was. It can take over my whole morning, honestly. So… yeah, I guess I am afraid of gaining weight, even a small amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, I do. I try to make it sound more reasonable in my head, like I’m just being “structured” or “staying disciplined,” but the honest version is that most days I’m definitely limiting it because I don’t want the number to go up.\n\nUsually I’ll just have coffee in the morning, because once I eat breakfast it feels like I’ve opened the door to the whole day going wrong. Then if I’m between sales calls and I’m getting shaky or distracted, I might have a protein bar, something I can count as contained and not a real meal. And then at night I’ll do something small, like grilled chicken, but I’ll skip the rice or anything that feels like it could make me look fuller the next day.\n\nIt’s not that I never get hungry. I do. But a full meal feels… I don’t know, it feels like I’ve messed up the day somehow. Like if I sit down and eat normally, then I’ve lost control, and then I’m going to have to deal with the consequences. So I keep it narrow, because that feels safer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if we’re being objective, yes. It’s been coming down over the past few months.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of why I’m here, or at least why other people pushed me to come here. HR made a comment—not in some official dramatic way at first, more like “Are you okay?”—and then my sister has been on me about it too. She keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, that I don’t look like myself. And I know they’re not saying it out of nowhere, but from my side it doesn’t land that way exactly.\n\nTo me, I look more put together. My suits fit sharper. I don’t feel like they’re pulling in the wrong places, and I look cleaner, more professional, if that makes sense. So when people say “too thin,” I hear that, but I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nThe weight has kept dropping since the divorce, though. I can say that. It wasn’t like one sudden drop overnight, but it’s been steady enough that people have noticed. I just… I don’t know, part of me thinks everyone is overreacting, and part of me knows that if HR and my sister are both bringing it up, maybe it’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you’re asking. I still do my sales route, I still show up, I still make the calls and see accounts and all of that. From the outside I probably look like I’m handling things fine.\n\nBut outside of that, I’ve gotten a lot more… selective, I guess. Or avoidant, if I’m being honest. Client dinners are a big one. If I don’t know the restaurant, or I can’t look at the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those shared-plates situations where everyone’s ordering for the table, I just don’t want to go. I’ll make excuses, like I have another meeting or I’m tired or I need to drive early the next morning.\n\nAnd it’s not just work-related stuff. Dating has gotten weird too. If someone suggests dinner or drinks somewhere I can’t control, I usually back out or try to steer it toward coffee or something “easy.” If I can’t control what the food situation is going to be, I get so preoccupied with it that I’m not even present anyway.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down invitations more. I keep more to myself than I used to. I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy or focused, but a lot of the time it’s really because I don’t want to deal with the food part.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be less attractive or acceptable to others if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds shallow, or like I’m saying everyone else is judging me that harshly, but that is kind of what goes through my head.\n\nBefore a meeting, or especially before a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I won’t really see the whole picture. I’ll just lock onto my stomach or my face. Like, if my shirt sits a certain way, or my face looks fuller that day, I start thinking I look soft. And then that turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m sure other people are going to see that as me being sloppy, or weak, or not as put together.\n\nIt’s not even always about attractiveness in a straightforward way, like “do I look good?” It’s more like… if I gained weight, I’d lose some kind of credibility. Like people would take me less seriously, or I’d seem less disciplined. And with dating, yeah, I worry they’d be less interested. Like whatever edge I have would be gone.\n\nI know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know people would probably say that. My sister definitely would. HR noticing it sort of tells me it’s not just in my head or some private thing. So, objectively, yeah, maybe I’m below what other people would call normal for my height and build.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to feel that way about it. Like, I’m still doing my job. I still drive my territory, I’m still seeing accounts, I’m still hitting my numbers. I can still get up and go for a jog. So when people talk like this is some major medical emergency, part of me just doesn’t connect with that. It feels dramatic, honestly.\n\nI’m not saying there’s nothing going on. I get that everyone is making a big deal about my weight for a reason. But in my own body, day to day, I don’t feel like someone who’s… I don’t know, falling apart. I feel functional. Maybe thinner than before, sure, but not like I’m at some crisis point the way other people seem to think.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or social events that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your concerns about weight or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, I guess I kind of already hinted at that, but yes.\n\nClient dinners are probably the clearest example. I used to actually like them, in a way — not always, because they can be exhausting, but it was part of the job and sometimes it was nice to sit down with an account and have a more normal conversation instead of just talking numbers or product. Now I’ll avoid them if I can’t control the food part. Like if I don’t know where we’re going, or I can’t look up the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those places where everyone orders a bunch of things to share, I get really uncomfortable. I’ll start thinking about it hours before, sometimes the whole day, and then I’m not even focused on the actual meeting.\n\nSo I’ve started making excuses. I’ll say I have an early drive the next morning, or I need to get back, or I already ate, or I’ll try to shift it to coffee instead. And it’s not like I can’t do my job — I still manage my sales route, I still see accounts, I still show up and do the work. But the social side of it has definitely shrunk because of this.\n\nDating too. I used to be more open to just going out to dinner or meeting someone wherever. Now if someone suggests a restaurant and I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering, I’ll either try to steer it somewhere else or I’ll just not go. I’ll tell myself I’m not that interested, or I’m too busy, but if I’m honest, a lot of the time it’s because I don’t want to deal with the menu or the pressure of eating in front of someone.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more than I used to. I don’t love saying that, because it makes it sound like my life is getting smaller, but… that’s probably true.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever engage in any behaviors to compensate for eating, such as self-induced vomiting or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, when you say “compensate,” I know what you’re asking, but I don’t really think of it in that clear cause-and-effect way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to do that.”\n\nI don’t make myself throw up, if that’s what you mean. That’s not something I do. And exercise… I do exercise, but I don’t know where the line is between normal and excessive. Sometimes if I’ve eaten more than I planned, I’ll feel really restless afterward, like I need to do something instead of just sitting there with it. But I don’t always label that as compensating. In my head it’s more like, “I should stay active,” or “I don’t want to feel gross,” which maybe sounds like the same thing from the outside.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no to vomiting. With exercise, I don’t know. I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who’s doing hours and hours every day or anything like that, but I do notice I get uncomfortable if I can’t move around after eating more than I meant to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what they are, obviously, but I don’t use them for that. I don’t have some routine where I’m taking pills or water pills or anything to make the number go down.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because when you ask it that way, I start second-guessing whether you mean prescription stuff, over-the-counter stuff, teas, supplements, all of that. But no — I’m not doing that as a weight-control thing. That’s not part of what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about weight are affecting your overall well-being or happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I don’t really like admitting that, because part of me wants to say, “I’m fine, I’m still functioning,” and technically I am. I still get up, I still manage my sales route, I still meet with accounts and do what I’m supposed to do for work. From the outside, it probably doesn’t look like everything has changed.\n\nBut it has affected my life. It’s like my whole day can start bending around whether I’ll be able to control what I eat, or what the options are going to be. Client dinners are the big one. I’ll avoid them unless I know the restaurant, or I can look at the menu ahead of time, or I feel like I can order something that feels… safe, I guess. If I can’t do that, I’ll come up with a reason not to go, or I’ll try to turn it into coffee or a quick meeting instead.\n\nAnd dating has gotten harder too. I’ll turn down plans or avoid making them if they involve dinner somewhere I don’t know. I tell myself it’s because I’m busy or not that interested, but a lot of the time it’s really about the food part. So I’ve been saying no to more things, keeping more to myself.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s affecting my happiness. Maybe not in some dramatic way every second, but my world feels smaller than it used to, and I don’t feel great about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of gaining weight is impacting your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I don’t always call it “fear of gaining weight” in the moment, because I sort of dress it up as being practical or busy or needing to stay on schedule, but if I’m being honest, it’s there.\n\nI can still do my job. I still manage my sales route, I still show up for accounts, I still get through the day. So part of me wants to say it hasn’t really stopped me from functioning. But the things around work — the social parts, the dinners, the after-hours stuff — those have gotten a lot harder. If a client suggests dinner and I don’t know the place, or I can’t look at the menu first, or I don’t feel like I’ll have control over what I order, I’ll find a way around it. I’ll say I have another appointment, or suggest coffee instead, or try to keep it to a meeting during the day.\n\nAnd it’s the same with dating, honestly. If it involves dinner somewhere I don’t know, I get tense about it before I even answer. I’ll turn things down that I probably would have enjoyed before, or I’ll avoid making plans unless I know I can control the food situation. So it’s not like I’m sitting at home all day unable to do anything, but I’ve definitely been saying no more. I keep more to myself. My life has gotten smaller in this way that I don’t really like admitting.\n\nDoctor: Are there any relationships in your life that have become strained or difficult because of your concerns about weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I wouldn’t say there’s been some huge blowup with anyone, like nobody has sat me down and said, “You’re ruining this relationship,” or anything like that. But things have gotten strained in a quieter way.\n\nWith work, I still manage my sales route and I still do what I’m supposed to do. I’m not missing accounts or anything. But the relationship side of the job — like client dinners, taking people out, saying yes when someone wants to continue a meeting over a meal — I avoid that more than I used to. If I don’t know the restaurant or I can’t check the menu first, I start looking for a way to get out of it. I’ll suggest coffee, or say I have another stop, or try to keep it to a daytime meeting. And I can tell sometimes that people notice. Not always in a direct way, but there’s a little awkwardness, like I’m less available or less easygoing than I used to be.\n\nAnd dating, yes, definitely. I turn down invitations if they involve dinner somewhere I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering. Or I’ll delay making plans, or try to steer it toward something safer. It’s embarrassing to say, because it sounds so rigid when I say it out loud, but it’s true. I’ve probably made myself seem uninterested or distant when really I was worried about the food part.\n\nSo I guess the strain is mostly that I’m saying no more. I’m harder to make plans with. I keep more to myself. It’s not that I don’t care about people — I do — but the menu and the situation around eating end up deciding whether I show up, and that’s made some relationships feel more distant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about weight are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I know I’ve kind of said this already, but yes, it is interfering. I’m still doing the basic things I have to do — like I still manage my sales route, I still see accounts, I still get through work — so in that sense I can tell myself it’s not that bad. I’m not just not showing up for my life completely.\n\nBut the truth is, a lot of my day-to-day choices are getting filtered through whether I can control the food situation. Client dinners are probably the clearest example. Those used to be a pretty normal part of my job, and now if someone suggests dinner, my first thought isn’t really about the client or the conversation — it’s, “Where is it? Can I see the menu? Is there something I can order that won’t feel like too much?” And if I can’t answer those questions, I start looking for a way out. I’ll suggest coffee, or say I have another commitment, or try to keep it to a regular meeting.\n\nAnd with dating it’s similar. I don’t really like admitting that, because it sounds… I don’t know, kind of controlled by something that should be simple. But if someone wants to go to dinner somewhere I don’t know, I’ll hesitate or turn it down, even if I might actually want to see them. I’ll tell myself I’m busy or it’s not a good night, but really it’s that I don’t want to be stuck with a menu I can’t manage.\n\nSo, yes, it’s made my life smaller. I’m turning down more invitations, avoiding things that involve meals, and keeping more to myself than I used to. It’s not like I can’t function at all, but I’m definitely not enjoying or participating in things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think probably yes. It’s hard to answer because part of me wants to say, “No, I’m just seeing what’s actually there,” but then I also know I get really fixated on certain parts in a way that maybe other people don’t.\n\nLike before a meeting or before a date, I’ll check myself in the mirror, and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my shirt sits a certain way or my face looks even a little fuller to me, then that becomes the only thing I can see. I’ll think I look soft, or kind of sloppy, even if objectively I’ve looked basically the same as I did the day before.\n\nAnd then I start assuming other people are going to see that too. Like a client will look at me and think I’m not disciplined, or I’m weak, or I don’t have myself together. With dating it’s even worse, because I’ll imagine the person noticing my stomach or my face and judging me before I’ve even walked in.\n\nI don’t know if other people actually see what I’m seeing. Nobody is saying those things to me. But in the moment, it feels very real, like it’s obvious and I’m just the one willing to admit it. Then later I can sometimes see that maybe I was over-focused on it, but when I’m standing there looking in the mirror, it’s hard to believe anyone else would see me differently.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Before a meeting, especially, or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check myself and I just sort of lock onto certain things—my stomach, my face. If my face looks fuller or my stomach looks soft, even a little, it gets in my head. I start thinking I look sloppy, like I don’t have myself together, or like people are going to read me as weak. And in my line of work, you’re constantly being judged, even if no one says it directly.\n\nSo when I’m at a weight where my face looks sharper and my clothes sit right and I don’t have that softness around my middle, then, yeah, I feel closer to what I’d call ideal. But I don’t know if that answers your question exactly, because it’s less “this is the ideal weight” and more… I feel okay when I don’t look soft.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like the word “afraid,” because it sounds dramatic, but if I’m being honest, yes, there’s definitely something there.\n\nLike if I go to a client dinner and I have to eat more than I planned, or I’m out for a weekend in Miami and there’s drinks and restaurants and everything is just less controlled, I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even a pound higher, it really gets to me. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, that’s normal fluctuation.” I know people say that, but in the moment it feels like I messed up or like I’m letting things slide.\n\nI get this kind of panic, like I’m losing control of myself. Then my head immediately starts going through what I need to do the next day to fix it—what I should cut back on, what I should avoid, how to get back to where I was. It can take over my whole morning, honestly. So… yeah, I guess I am afraid of gaining weight, even a small amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, I do. I try to make it sound more reasonable in my head, like I’m just being “structured” or “staying disciplined,” but the honest version is that most days I’m definitely limiting it because I don’t want the number to go up.\n\nUsually I’ll just have coffee in the morning, because once I eat breakfast it feels like I’ve opened the door to the whole day going wrong. Then if I’m between sales calls and I’m getting shaky or distracted, I might have a protein bar, something I can count as contained and not a real meal. And then at night I’ll do something small, like grilled chicken, but I’ll skip the rice or anything that feels like it could make me look fuller the next day.\n\nIt’s not that I never get hungry. I do. But a full meal feels… I don’t know, it feels like I’ve messed up the day somehow. Like if I sit down and eat normally, then I’ve lost control, and then I’m going to have to deal with the consequences. So I keep it narrow, because that feels safer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if we’re being objective, yes. It’s been coming down over the past few months.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of why I’m here, or at least why other people pushed me to come here. HR made a comment—not in some official dramatic way at first, more like “Are you okay?”—and then my sister has been on me about it too. She keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, that I don’t look like myself. And I know they’re not saying it out of nowhere, but from my side it doesn’t land that way exactly.\n\nTo me, I look more put together. My suits fit sharper. I don’t feel like they’re pulling in the wrong places, and I look cleaner, more professional, if that makes sense. So when people say “too thin,” I hear that, but I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nThe weight has kept dropping since the divorce, though. I can say that. It wasn’t like one sudden drop overnight, but it’s been steady enough that people have noticed. I just… I don’t know, part of me thinks everyone is overreacting, and part of me knows that if HR and my sister are both bringing it up, maybe it’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you’re asking. I still do my sales route, I still show up, I still make the calls and see accounts and all of that. From the outside I probably look like I’m handling things fine.\n\nBut outside of that, I’ve gotten a lot more… selective, I guess. Or avoidant, if I’m being honest. Client dinners are a big one. If I don’t know the restaurant, or I can’t look at the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those shared-plates situations where everyone’s ordering for the table, I just don’t want to go. I’ll make excuses, like I have another meeting or I’m tired or I need to drive early the next morning.\n\nAnd it’s not just work-related stuff. Dating has gotten weird too. If someone suggests dinner or drinks somewhere I can’t control, I usually back out or try to steer it toward coffee or something “easy.” If I can’t control what the food situation is going to be, I get so preoccupied with it that I’m not even present anyway.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down invitations more. I keep more to myself than I used to. I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy or focused, but a lot of the time it’s really because I don’t want to deal with the food part.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be less attractive or acceptable to others if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds shallow, or like I’m saying everyone else is judging me that harshly, but that is kind of what goes through my head.\n\nBefore a meeting, or especially before a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I won’t really see the whole picture. I’ll just lock onto my stomach or my face. Like, if my shirt sits a certain way, or my face looks fuller that day, I start thinking I look soft. And then that turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m sure other people are going to see that as me being sloppy, or weak, or not as put together.\n\nIt’s not even always about attractiveness in a straightforward way, like “do I look good?” It’s more like… if I gained weight, I’d lose some kind of credibility. Like people would take me less seriously, or I’d seem less disciplined. And with dating, yeah, I worry they’d be less interested. Like whatever edge I have would be gone.\n\nI know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know people would probably say that. My sister definitely would. HR noticing it sort of tells me it’s not just in my head or some private thing. So, objectively, yeah, maybe I’m below what other people would call normal for my height and build.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to feel that way about it. Like, I’m still doing my job. I still drive my territory, I’m still seeing accounts, I’m still hitting my numbers. I can still get up and go for a jog. So when people talk like this is some major medical emergency, part of me just doesn’t connect with that. It feels dramatic, honestly.\n\nI’m not saying there’s nothing going on. I get that everyone is making a big deal about my weight for a reason. But in my own body, day to day, I don’t feel like someone who’s… I don’t know, falling apart. I feel functional. Maybe thinner than before, sure, but not like I’m at some crisis point the way other people seem to think.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or social events that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your concerns about weight or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, I guess I kind of already hinted at that, but yes.\n\nClient dinners are probably the clearest example. I used to actually like them, in a way — not always, because they can be exhausting, but it was part of the job and sometimes it was nice to sit down with an account and have a more normal conversation instead of just talking numbers or product. Now I’ll avoid them if I can’t control the food part. Like if I don’t know where we’re going, or I can’t look up the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those places where everyone orders a bunch of things to share, I get really uncomfortable. I’ll start thinking about it hours before, sometimes the whole day, and then I’m not even focused on the actual meeting.\n\nSo I’ve started making excuses. I’ll say I have an early drive the next morning, or I need to get back, or I already ate, or I’ll try to shift it to coffee instead. And it’s not like I can’t do my job — I still manage my sales route, I still see accounts, I still show up and do the work. But the social side of it has definitely shrunk because of this.\n\nDating too. I used to be more open to just going out to dinner or meeting someone wherever. Now if someone suggests a restaurant and I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering, I’ll either try to steer it somewhere else or I’ll just not go. I’ll tell myself I’m not that interested, or I’m too busy, but if I’m honest, a lot of the time it’s because I don’t want to deal with the menu or the pressure of eating in front of someone.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more than I used to. I don’t love saying that, because it makes it sound like my life is getting smaller, but… that’s probably true.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever engage in any behaviors to compensate for eating, such as self-induced vomiting or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, when you say “compensate,” I know what you’re asking, but I don’t really think of it in that clear cause-and-effect way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to do that.”\n\nI don’t make myself throw up, if that’s what you mean. That’s not something I do. And exercise… I do exercise, but I don’t know where the line is between normal and excessive. Sometimes if I’ve eaten more than I planned, I’ll feel really restless afterward, like I need to do something instead of just sitting there with it. But I don’t always label that as compensating. In my head it’s more like, “I should stay active,” or “I don’t want to feel gross,” which maybe sounds like the same thing from the outside.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no to vomiting. With exercise, I don’t know. I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who’s doing hours and hours every day or anything like that, but I do notice I get uncomfortable if I can’t move around after eating more than I meant to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what they are, obviously, but I don’t use them for that. I don’t have some routine where I’m taking pills or water pills or anything to make the number go down.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because when you ask it that way, I start second-guessing whether you mean prescription stuff, over-the-counter stuff, teas, supplements, all of that. But no — I’m not doing that as a weight-control thing. That’s not part of what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about weight are affecting your overall well-being or happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I don’t really like admitting that, because part of me wants to say, “I’m fine, I’m still functioning,” and technically I am. I still get up, I still manage my sales route, I still meet with accounts and do what I’m supposed to do for work. From the outside, it probably doesn’t look like everything has changed.\n\nBut it has affected my life. It’s like my whole day can start bending around whether I’ll be able to control what I eat, or what the options are going to be. Client dinners are the big one. I’ll avoid them unless I know the restaurant, or I can look at the menu ahead of time, or I feel like I can order something that feels… safe, I guess. If I can’t do that, I’ll come up with a reason not to go, or I’ll try to turn it into coffee or a quick meeting instead.\n\nAnd dating has gotten harder too. I’ll turn down plans or avoid making them if they involve dinner somewhere I don’t know. I tell myself it’s because I’m busy or not that interested, but a lot of the time it’s really about the food part. So I’ve been saying no to more things, keeping more to myself.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s affecting my happiness. Maybe not in some dramatic way every second, but my world feels smaller than it used to, and I don’t feel great about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of gaining weight is impacting your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I don’t always call it “fear of gaining weight” in the moment, because I sort of dress it up as being practical or busy or needing to stay on schedule, but if I’m being honest, it’s there.\n\nI can still do my job. I still manage my sales route, I still show up for accounts, I still get through the day. So part of me wants to say it hasn’t really stopped me from functioning. But the things around work — the social parts, the dinners, the after-hours stuff — those have gotten a lot harder. If a client suggests dinner and I don’t know the place, or I can’t look at the menu first, or I don’t feel like I’ll have control over what I order, I’ll find a way around it. I’ll say I have another appointment, or suggest coffee instead, or try to keep it to a meeting during the day.\n\nAnd it’s the same with dating, honestly. If it involves dinner somewhere I don’t know, I get tense about it before I even answer. I’ll turn things down that I probably would have enjoyed before, or I’ll avoid making plans unless I know I can control the food situation. So it’s not like I’m sitting at home all day unable to do anything, but I’ve definitely been saying no more. I keep more to myself. My life has gotten smaller in this way that I don’t really like admitting.\n\nDoctor: Are there any relationships in your life that have become strained or difficult because of your concerns about weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I wouldn’t say there’s been some huge blowup with anyone, like nobody has sat me down and said, “You’re ruining this relationship,” or anything like that. But things have gotten strained in a quieter way.\n\nWith work, I still manage my sales route and I still do what I’m supposed to do. I’m not missing accounts or anything. But the relationship side of the job — like client dinners, taking people out, saying yes when someone wants to continue a meeting over a meal — I avoid that more than I used to. If I don’t know the restaurant or I can’t check the menu first, I start looking for a way to get out of it. I’ll suggest coffee, or say I have another stop, or try to keep it to a daytime meeting. And I can tell sometimes that people notice. Not always in a direct way, but there’s a little awkwardness, like I’m less available or less easygoing than I used to be.\n\nAnd dating, yes, definitely. I turn down invitations if they involve dinner somewhere I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering. Or I’ll delay making plans, or try to steer it toward something safer. It’s embarrassing to say, because it sounds so rigid when I say it out loud, but it’s true. I’ve probably made myself seem uninterested or distant when really I was worried about the food part.\n\nSo I guess the strain is mostly that I’m saying no more. I’m harder to make plans with. I keep more to myself. It’s not that I don’t care about people — I do — but the menu and the situation around eating end up deciding whether I show up, and that’s made some relationships feel more distant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about weight are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I know I’ve kind of said this already, but yes, it is interfering. I’m still doing the basic things I have to do — like I still manage my sales route, I still see accounts, I still get through work — so in that sense I can tell myself it’s not that bad. I’m not just not showing up for my life completely.\n\nBut the truth is, a lot of my day-to-day choices are getting filtered through whether I can control the food situation. Client dinners are probably the clearest example. Those used to be a pretty normal part of my job, and now if someone suggests dinner, my first thought isn’t really about the client or the conversation — it’s, “Where is it? Can I see the menu? Is there something I can order that won’t feel like too much?” And if I can’t answer those questions, I start looking for a way out. I’ll suggest coffee, or say I have another commitment, or try to keep it to a regular meeting.\n\nAnd with dating it’s similar. I don’t really like admitting that, because it sounds… I don’t know, kind of controlled by something that should be simple. But if someone wants to go to dinner somewhere I don’t know, I’ll hesitate or turn it down, even if I might actually want to see them. I’ll tell myself I’m busy or it’s not a good night, but really it’s that I don’t want to be stuck with a menu I can’t manage.\n\nSo, yes, it’s made my life smaller. I’m turning down more invitations, avoiding things that involve meals, and keeping more to myself than I used to. It’s not like I can’t function at all, but I’m definitely not enjoying or participating in things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think probably yes. It’s hard to answer because part of me wants to say, “No, I’m just seeing what’s actually there,” but then I also know I get really fixated on certain parts in a way that maybe other people don’t.\n\nLike before a meeting or before a date, I’ll check myself in the mirror, and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my shirt sits a certain way or my face looks even a little fuller to me, then that becomes the only thing I can see. I’ll think I look soft, or kind of sloppy, even if objectively I’ve looked basically the same as I did the day before.\n\nAnd then I start assuming other people are going to see that too. Like a client will look at me and think I’m not disciplined, or I’m weak, or I don’t have myself together. With dating it’s even worse, because I’ll imagine the person noticing my stomach or my face and judging me before I’ve even walked in.\n\nI don’t know if other people actually see what I’m seeing. Nobody is saying those things to me. But in the moment, it feels very real, like it’s obvious and I’m just the one willing to admit it. Then later I can sometimes see that maybe I was over-focused on it, but when I’m standing there looking in the mirror, it’s hard to believe anyone else would see me differently.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I turn down invitations if they involve dinner somewhere I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering. Or I’ll delay making plans, or try to steer it toward something safer. It’s embarrassing to say, because it sounds so rigid when I say it out loud, but it’s true. I’ve probably made myself seem uninterested or distant when really I was worried about the food part.\n\nSo I guess the strain is mostly that I’m saying no more. I’m harder to make plans with. I keep more to myself. It’s not that I don’t care about people — I do — but the menu and the situation around eating end up deciding whether I show up, and that’s made some relationships feel more distant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about weight are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I know I’ve kind of said this already, but yes, it is interfering. I’m still doing the basic things I have to do — like I still manage my sales route, I still see accounts, I still get through work — so in that sense I can tell myself it’s not that bad. I’m not just not showing up for my life completely.\n\nBut the truth is, a lot of my day-to-day choices are getting filtered through whether I can control the food situation. Client dinners are probably the clearest example. Those used to be a pretty normal part of my job, and now if someone suggests dinner, my first thought isn’t really about the client or the conversation — it’s, “Where is it? Can I see the menu? Is there something I can order that won’t feel like too much?” And if I can’t answer those questions, I start looking for a way out. I’ll suggest coffee, or say I have another commitment, or try to keep it to a regular meeting.\n\nAnd with dating it’s similar. I don’t really like admitting that, because it sounds… I don’t know, kind of controlled by something that should be simple. But if someone wants to go to dinner somewhere I don’t know, I’ll hesitate or turn it down, even if I might actually want to see them. I’ll tell myself I’m busy or it’s not a good night, but really it’s that I don’t want to be stuck with a menu I can’t manage.\n\nSo, yes, it’s made my life smaller. I’m turning down more invitations, avoiding things that involve meals, and keeping more to myself than I used to. It’s not like I can’t function at all, but I’m definitely not enjoying or participating in things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think probably yes. It’s hard to answer because part of me wants to say, “No, I’m just seeing what’s actually there,” but then I also know I get really fixated on certain parts in a way that maybe other people don’t.\n\nLike before a meeting or before a date, I’ll check myself in the mirror, and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my shirt sits a certain way or my face looks even a little fuller to me, then that becomes the only thing I can see. I’ll think I look soft, or kind of sloppy, even if objectively I’ve looked basically the same as I did the day before.\n\nAnd then I start assuming other people are going to see that too. Like a client will look at me and think I’m not disciplined, or I’m weak, or I don’t have myself together. With dating it’s even worse, because I’ll imagine the person noticing my stomach or my face and judging me before I’ve even walked in.\n\nI don’t know if other people actually see what I’m seeing. Nobody is saying those things to me. But in the moment, it feels very real, like it’s obvious and I’m just the one willing to admit it.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean… I’ve had times where I’ve eaten something and felt really bad about it afterward, like guilty or uncomfortable with myself, but when you say “a large amount of food in a short period of time,” I don’t know that that’s really what happens.

It’s more like I’ll be very aware of what I ate, and then I’ll replay it in my head and feel like I shouldn’t have had it, or I should have chosen differently. But I don’t think I have these episodes where I’m eating a huge amount all at once and can’t stop. At least, not that I would call it that.

I guess I’m hesitating because my version of “too much” might be kind of skewed. Like something can feel like a lot to me even if someone else would probably say it was a normal amount. So the guilt part, yes, I can relate to that. But the binge-type part — eating a large amount quickly — I don’t think so.

I figured coming in might at least get them to stop worrying so much, or… I don’t know, help me understand if they’re overreacting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… underweight compared to what, I guess? Like if I sit down and eat normally, then I’ve lost control, and then I’m going to have to deal with the consequences. HR made a comment—not in some official dramatic way at first, more like “Are you okay?”—and then my sister has been on me about it too. She keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, that I don’t look like myself. I just… I don’t know, part of me thinks everyone is overreacting, and part of me knows that if HR and my sister are both bringing it up, maybe it’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you’re asking. I still do my sales route, I still show up, I still make the calls and see accounts and all of that. I keep more to myself than I used to. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds shallow, or like I’m saying everyone else is judging me that harshly, but that is kind of what goes through my head.\n\nBefore a meeting, or especially before a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I won’t really see the whole picture. But the relationship side of the job — like client dinners, taking people out, saying yes when someone wants to continue a meeting over a meal — I avoid that more than I used to. If I don’t know the restaurant or I can’t check the menu first, I start looking for a way to get out of it. I’ll suggest coffee, or say I have another stop, or try to keep it to a daytime meeting. And I can tell sometimes that people notice. Not always in a direct way, but there’s a little awkwardness, like I’m less available or less easygoing than I used to be.\n\nAnd dating, yes, definitely. I turn down invitations if they involve dinner somewhere I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering. Or I’ll delay making plans, or try to steer it toward something safer. It’s embarrassing to say, because it sounds so rigid when I say it out loud, but it’s true. I’ve probably made myself seem uninterested or distant when really I was worried about the food part.\n\nSo I guess the strain is mostly that I’m saying no more. I’m harder to make plans with. I keep more to myself. It’s not that I don’t care about people — I do — but the menu and the situation around eating end up deciding whether I show up, and that’s made some relationships feel more distant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about weight are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I know I’ve kind of said this already, but yes, it is interfering. I’m still doing the basic things I have to do — like I still manage my sales route, I still see accounts, I still get through work — so in that sense I can tell myself it’s not that bad. I’m not just not showing up for my life completely.\n\nBut the truth is, a lot of my day-to-day choices are getting filtered through whether I can control the food situation. Client dinners are probably the clearest example. Those used to be a pretty normal part of my job, and now if someone suggests dinner, my first thought isn’t really about the client or the conversation — it’s, “Where is it? Can I see the menu? Is there something I can order that won’t feel like too much?” And if I can’t answer those questions, I start looking for a way out. I’ll suggest coffee, or say I have another commitment, or try to keep it to a regular meeting.\n\nAnd with dating it’s similar. I don’t really like admitting that, because it sounds… I don’t know, kind of controlled by something that should be simple. But if someone wants to go to dinner somewhere I don’t know, I’ll hesitate or turn it down, even if I might actually want to see them. I’ll tell myself I’m busy or it’s not a good night, but really it’s that I don’t want to be stuck with a menu I can’t manage.\n\nSo, yes, it’s made my life smaller. I’m turning down more invitations, avoiding things that involve meals, and keeping more to myself than I used to. It’s not like I can’t function at all, but I’m definitely not enjoying or participating in things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think probably yes. It’s hard to answer because part of me wants to say, “No, I’m just seeing what’s actually there,” but then I also know I get really fixated on certain parts in a way that maybe other people don’t.\n\nLike before a meeting or before a date, I’ll check myself in the mirror, and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my shirt sits a certain way or my face looks even a little fuller to me, then that becomes the only thing I can see. I’ll think I look soft, or kind of sloppy, even if objectively I’ve looked basically the same as I did the day before.\n\nAnd then I start assuming other people are going to see that too. Nobody is saying those things to me. But in the moment, it feels very real, like it’s obvious and I’m just the one willing to admit it. Then later I can sometimes see that maybe I was over-focused on it, but when I’m standing there looking in the mirror, it’s hard to believe anyone else would see me differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, followed by feelings of guilt or shame?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean… I’ve had times where I’ve eaten something and felt really bad about it afterward, like guilty or uncomfortable with myself, but when you say “a large amount of food in a short period of time,” I don’t know that that’s really what happens.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be very aware of what I ate, and then I’ll replay it in my head and feel like I shouldn’t have had it, or I should have chosen differently. But I don’t think I have these episodes where I’m eating a huge amount all at once and can’t stop. At least, not that I would call it that.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because my version of “too much” might be kind of skewed. Like something can feel like a lot to me even if someone else would probably say it was a normal amount. So the guilt part, yes, I can relate to that. But the binge-type part — eating a large amount quickly — I don’t think so.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in the way I think you mean. I don’t usually feel like I start eating and then suddenly can’t stop myself.

If anything, I’m usually very aware the whole time — maybe too aware — of what I’m eating, how much is left, whether I should keep going, whether I’ll regret it afterward. So it’s not like I go into this mode where I lose control and just keep eating without being able to stop.

I guess there are moments where I’ll think, “I already started, so now I’ve messed up,” and that can make me feel anxious or kind of guilty, but I still don’t think I’d say I can’t stop. It’s more mental than that, like I’m arguing with myself the whole time. But no, I don’t really have episodes where I feel out of control with eating once I’ve started.

HR made a comment—not in some official dramatic way at first, more like “Are you okay?”—and then my sister has been on me about it too. She keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, that I don’t look like myself. I turn down invitations if they involve dinner somewhere I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering. Or I’ll delay making plans, or try to steer it toward something safer. I’ll suggest coffee, or say I have another commitment, or try to keep it to a regular meeting.\n\nAnd with dating it’s similar. I don’t really like admitting that, because it sounds… I don’t know, kind of controlled by something that should be simple. But if someone wants to go to dinner somewhere I don’t know, I’ll hesitate or turn it down, even if I might actually want to see them. I’ll tell myself I’m busy or it’s not a good night, but really it’s that I don’t want to be stuck with a menu I can’t manage.\n\nSo, yes, it’s made my life smaller. I’m turning down more invitations, avoiding things that involve meals, and keeping more to myself than I used to. It’s not like I can’t function at all, but I’m definitely not enjoying or participating in things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think probably yes. It’s hard to answer because part of me wants to say, “No, I’m just seeing what’s actually there,” but then I also know I get really fixated on certain parts in a way that maybe other people don’t.\n\nLike before a meeting or before a date, I’ll check myself in the mirror, and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my shirt sits a certain way or my face looks even a little fuller to me, then that becomes the only thing I can see. I’ll think I look soft, or kind of sloppy, even if objectively I’ve looked basically the same as I did the day before.\n\nAnd then I start assuming other people are going to see that too. Like a client will look at me and think I’m not disciplined, or I’m weak, or I don’t have myself together. With dating it’s even worse, because I’ll imagine the person noticing my stomach or my face and judging me before I’ve even walked in.\n\nI don’t know if other people actually see what I’m seeing. Nobody is saying those things to me. But in the moment, it feels very real, like it’s obvious and I’m just the one willing to admit it. Then later I can sometimes see that maybe I was over-focused on it, but when I’m standing there looking in the mirror, it’s hard to believe anyone else would see me differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, followed by feelings of guilt or shame?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean… I’ve had times where I’ve eaten something and felt really bad about it afterward, like guilty or uncomfortable with myself, but when you say “a large amount of food in a short period of time,” I don’t know that that’s really what happens.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be very aware of what I ate, and then I’ll replay it in my head and feel like I shouldn’t have had it, or I should have chosen differently. But I don’t think I have these episodes where I’m eating a huge amount all at once and can’t stop. At least, not that I would call it that.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because my version of “too much” might be kind of skewed. Like something can feel like a lot to me even if someone else would probably say it was a normal amount. So the guilt part, yes, I can relate to that. But the binge-type part — eating a large amount quickly — I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Not really in the way I think you mean. I don’t usually feel like I start eating and then suddenly can’t stop myself.\n\nIf anything, I’m usually very aware the whole time — maybe too aware — of what I’m eating, how much is left, whether I should keep going, whether I’ll regret it afterward. So it’s not like I go into this mode where I lose control and just keep eating without being able to stop.\n\nI guess there are moments where I’ll think, “I already started, so now I’ve messed up,” and that can make me feel anxious or kind of guilty, but I still don’t think I’d say I can’t stop. It’s more mental than that, like I’m arguing with myself the whole time. But no, I don’t really have episodes where I feel out of control with eating once I’ve started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

sales_representative_wholesale_or_manufacturing / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S044\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others But I don’t think I have these episodes where I’m eating a huge amount all at once and can’t stop. At least, not that I would call it that.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because my version of “too much” might be kind of skewed. Like something can feel like a lot to me even if someone else would probably say it was a normal amount. So the guilt part, yes, I can relate to that. But the binge-type part — eating a large amount quickly — I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Not really in the way I think you mean. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, not in the sense of, like, a really large amount in a short time. I keep getting stuck on the word “excessive,” because to me something can feel excessive even if I know, logically, another person might look at it and say it wasn’t much at all.

So if you mean do I sit down and eat, like, a huge amount quickly, then no, I don’t really think that happens. I’m usually very conscious of what I’m eating while I’m eating it. But if you mean do I sometimes feel afterward like I had “too much,” then yes, I can feel that way — though I’m not sure my sense of “too much” is very reliable.

It’s more that I’ll eat something and then start analyzing it, like whether it was necessary, whether it was more than I should have had, whether it’s going to show somehow. But I wouldn’t say it’s an episode of eating an objectively excessive amount in a short period. At least not that I can identify.

They keep saying I’ve gotten too thin, like it’s become this whole thing other people are noticing, and I guess it’s gotten hard to brush off when it’s coming from both work and family.\n\nFrom my side, though, I don’t really see it the same way. But I don’t know if that answers your question exactly, because it’s less “this is the ideal weight” and more… I feel okay when I don’t look soft.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like the word “afraid,” because it sounds dramatic, but if I’m being honest, yes, there’s definitely something there.\n\nLike if I go to a client dinner and I have to eat more than I planned, or I’m out for a weekend in Miami and there’s drinks and restaurants and everything is just less controlled, I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even a pound higher, it really gets to me. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, that’s normal fluctuation.” I know people say that, but in the moment it feels like I messed up or like I’m letting things slide.\n\nI get this kind of panic, like I’m losing control of myself. Then my head immediately starts going through what I need to do the next day to fix it—what I should cut back on, what I should avoid, how to get back to where I was. It can take over my whole morning, honestly. So… yeah, I guess I am afraid of gaining weight, even a small amount.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, I do. I try to make it sound more reasonable in my head, like I’m just being “structured” or “staying disciplined,” but the honest version is that most days I’m definitely limiting it because I don’t want the number to go up.\n\nUsually I’ll just have coffee in the morning, because once I eat breakfast it feels like I’ve opened the door to the whole day going wrong. Then if I’m between sales calls and I’m getting shaky or distracted, I might have a protein bar, something I can count as contained and not a real meal. And then at night I’ll do something small, like grilled chicken, but I’ll skip the rice or anything that feels like it could make me look fuller the next day.\n\nIt’s not that I never get hungry. I do. But a full meal feels… I don’t know, it feels like I’ve messed up the day somehow. Like if I sit down and eat normally, then I’ve lost control, and then I’m going to have to deal with the consequences. So I keep it narrow, because that feels safer.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if we’re being objective, yes. It’s been coming down over the past few months.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of why I’m here, or at least why other people pushed me to come here. HR made a comment—not in some official dramatic way at first, more like “Are you okay?”—and then my sister has been on me about it too. She keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin, that I don’t look like myself. And I know they’re not saying it out of nowhere, but from my side it doesn’t land that way exactly.\n\nTo me, I look more put together. My suits fit sharper. I don’t feel like they’re pulling in the wrong places, and I look cleaner, more professional, if that makes sense. So when people say “too thin,” I hear that, but I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nThe weight has kept dropping since the divorce, though. I can say that. It wasn’t like one sudden drop overnight, but it’s been steady enough that people have noticed. I just… I don’t know, part of me thinks everyone is overreacting, and part of me knows that if HR and my sister are both bringing it up, maybe it’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you’re asking. I still do my sales route, I still show up, I still make the calls and see accounts and all of that. From the outside I probably look like I’m handling things fine.\n\nBut outside of that, I’ve gotten a lot more… selective, I guess. Or avoidant, if I’m being honest. Client dinners are a big one. If I don’t know the restaurant, or I can’t look at the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those shared-plates situations where everyone’s ordering for the table, I just don’t want to go. I’ll make excuses, like I have another meeting or I’m tired or I need to drive early the next morning.\n\nAnd it’s not just work-related stuff. Dating has gotten weird too. If someone suggests dinner or drinks somewhere I can’t control, I usually back out or try to steer it toward coffee or something “easy.” If I can’t control what the food situation is going to be, I get so preoccupied with it that I’m not even present anyway.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down invitations more. I keep more to myself than I used to. I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy or focused, but a lot of the time it’s really because I don’t want to deal with the food part.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be less attractive or acceptable to others if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds shallow, or like I’m saying everyone else is judging me that harshly, but that is kind of what goes through my head.\n\nBefore a meeting, or especially before a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I won’t really see the whole picture. I’ll just lock onto my stomach or my face. Like, if my shirt sits a certain way, or my face looks fuller that day, I start thinking I look soft. And then that turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m sure other people are going to see that as me being sloppy, or weak, or not as put together.\n\nIt’s not even always about attractiveness in a straightforward way, like “do I look good?” It’s more like… if I gained weight, I’d lose some kind of credibility. Like people would take me less seriously, or I’d seem less disciplined. And with dating, yeah, I worry they’d be less interested. Like whatever edge I have would be gone.\n\nI know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know people would probably say that. My sister definitely would. HR noticing it sort of tells me it’s not just in my head or some private thing. So, objectively, yeah, maybe I’m below what other people would call normal for my height and build.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to feel that way about it. Like, I’m still doing my job. I still drive my territory, I’m still seeing accounts, I’m still hitting my numbers. I can still get up and go for a jog. I get that everyone is making a big deal about my weight for a reason. But in my own body, day to day, I don’t feel like someone who’s… I don’t know, falling apart. I feel functional. Maybe thinner than before, sure, but not like I’m at some crisis point the way other people seem to think.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or social events that you used to enjoy but now avoid because of your concerns about weight or eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Now I’ll avoid them if I can’t control the food part. Like if I don’t know where we’re going, or I can’t look up the menu ahead of time, or it’s one of those places where everyone orders a bunch of things to share, I get really uncomfortable. I’ll start thinking about it hours before, sometimes the whole day, and then I’m not even focused on the actual meeting.\n\nSo I’ve started making excuses. I’ll say I have an early drive the next morning, or I need to get back, or I already ate, or I’ll try to shift it to coffee instead. And it’s not like I can’t do my job — I still manage my sales route, I still see accounts, I still show up and do the work. But the social side of it has definitely shrunk because of this.\n\nDating too. I used to be more open to just going out to dinner or meeting someone wherever. Now if someone suggests a restaurant and I don’t feel like I can control what I’m ordering, I’ll either try to steer it somewhere else or I’ll just not go. I’ll tell myself I’m not that interested, or I’m too busy, but if I’m honest, a lot of the time it’s because I don’t want to deal with the menu or the pressure of eating in front of someone.\n\nSo yeah, I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more than I used to. I don’t love saying that, because it makes it sound like my life is getting smaller, but… that’s probably true.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever engage in any behaviors to compensate for eating, such as self-induced vomiting or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, when you say “compensate,” I know what you’re asking, but I don’t really think of it in that clear cause-and-effect way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to do that.”\n\nI don’t make myself throw up, if that’s what you mean. That’s not something I do. And exercise… I do exercise, but I don’t know where the line is between normal and excessive. Sometimes if I’ve eaten more than I planned, I’ll feel really restless afterward, like I need to do something instead of just sitting there with it. But I don’t always label that as compensating. In my head it’s more like, “I should stay active,” or “I don’t want to feel gross,” which maybe sounds like the same thing from the outside.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no to vomiting. With exercise, I don’t know. I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who’s doing hours and hours every day or anything like that, but I do notice I get uncomfortable if I can’t move around after eating more than I meant to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know what they are, obviously, but I don’t use them for that. I don’t have some routine where I’m taking pills or water pills or anything to make the number go down.\n\nI guess I’m hesitating because when you ask it that way, I start second-guessing whether you mean prescription stuff, over-the-counter stuff, teas, supplements, all of that. But no — I’m not doing that as a weight-control thing. That’s not part of what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about weight are affecting your overall well-being or happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I don’t really like admitting that, because part of me wants to say, “I’m fine, I’m still functioning,” and technically I am. I still get up, I still manage my sales route, I still meet with accounts and do what I’m supposed to do for work. From the outside, it probably doesn’t look like everything has changed.\n\nBut it has affected my life. It’s like my whole day can start bending around whether I’ll be able to control what I eat, or what the options are going to be. Client dinners are the big one. I’ll avoid them unless I know the restaurant, or I can look at the menu ahead of time, or I feel like I can order something that feels… safe, I guess. If I can’t do that, I’ll come up with a reason not to go, or I’ll try to turn it into coffee or a quick meeting instead.\n\nAnd dating has gotten harder too. I’ll turn down plans or avoid making them if they involve dinner somewhere I don’t know. I tell myself it’s because I’m busy or not that interested, but a lot of the time it’s really about the food part. So I’ve been saying no to more things, keeping more to myself.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s affecting my happiness.
